| **[Toward Unified Robot Learning: Bridging Representation, Vision-Language-Action, and World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03927v1)** | 2026-09-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>For robots to operate reliably in real-world environments, they need to perceive their surroundings, act, and reason about the consequences of those actions. Rapid progress in the domains of representation learning, VLA models, and world models has significantly enhanced the capabilities of robot learning systems, enabling robots to work in increasingly complex environments. However, these paradigms are typically developed in isolation, resulting in fragmented systems that struggle with generalization, long-horizon temporal reasoning and planning, and deployment in unstructured environments. In this survey, we present a unified perspective on robot learning by organizing the existing methods along three complementary axes: understanding through representation learning, acting through VLA models, and reasoning through world models. We introduce a structured taxonomy that captures key design choices in environment representation, policy learning, and predictive modeling, and summarize the recent progress in these domains. Beyond classifying the existing works, we analyze how these components interact, discuss common limitations, and highlight emerging trends towards more integrated systems. Through this lens, we identify the challenges in the domain of robot learning, including uncertainty quantification, out-of-distribution generalization, cross-embodiment transfer, long-context understanding, and long-horizon planning. We argue that these challenges arise not only from limitations within individual components but also from the lack of integration across perception, action, and reasoning. Building on this analysis, we outline future directions towards unified, physically grounded, and probabilistic robot learning to develop robust robotic systems that maintain consistent internal representations and support decision making over extended interactions in real-world environments.</p></details> |  |

Our key idea is to virtually adjust test-time camera observations to match the training camera configuration in real-time. We further validate that our approach constantly enhances viewpoint robustness in real-world robotic manipulation scenarios, including settings with varying camera extrinsics, intrinsics, and freely moving handheld cameras. Project Page: https://heo0224.github.io/AnyCamVLA/</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to IROS 2026</p></details> |

### VLM for Navigation and Localization
> Vision-language models, visual foundation models, localization, and map-aware navigation.

| **[ArchiveLens: A Generative Spatial Knowledge Framework for Semantic Reconstruction of Historical Maps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35606v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Historical maps contain dense morphological and semantic information about past urban environments, but many digitization workflows focus primarily on extracting geometry, and do not capture cartographic semantics such as building use and construction materials. This study introduces ArchiveLens, a generative spatial knowledge framework that operationalizes historical maps as entity-centric, machine-queryable geospatial databases. The pipeline decouples morphological and semantic reconstruction through three stages: tiled YOLOv11-seg instance segmentation for building footprint extraction; deterministic material classification via hue, saturation, and value (HSV)-based color thresholds; and localized attribute parsing using a quantized vision-language model constrained by domain-specific prompts. Applied to 445 georeferenced Sanborn map sheets of Chicago (1918-1950), the framework achieves high geometric and semantic accuracy. Beyond performance, ArchiveLens advances GIScience by formalizing historical cartography as structured spatial knowledge representation, allowing researchers to query building attributes and compare spatial patterns across map sheets and years.</p></details> | 31 pages |
| **[EARS: Evidence-Aware Retrieval via Sound and Motion for Budgeted Video Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35536v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Uniform frame sampling can miss brief evidence in sparse-event videos when the VLM sees only K frames. The evidence score alone has frame-level AP 0.39; used only to select frames for the VLM, EARS with temporal diversity reaches localization AP 0.94, compared with 0.88 under Uniform sampling. In the updated matched study, at K = 1 EARS reaches XD-subset F1 0.660 versus 0.614 for centered Uniform (the paired interval includes zero); earlier backbone studies use separate protocols. On mostly-silent UCF-Crime, however, EARS slightly trails Uniform. A single configuration is used across both benchmarks; the new controls do not repeat the original held-out search. EARS therefore targets low-budget videos whose salient evidence aligns with motion or audio; on dense narratives or stealthy events, uniform or semantic selection remains preferable, and cheap modality statistics cannot reliably route between them. | **[Shared Context, Separate Engines: Streaming Vision--Language Inference on Edge SoCs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35309v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Edge SoCs pack CPUs, GPUs, and NPUs, yet vision--language model (VLM) runtimes keep generation on one engine and leave the others idle. Streaming VLMs expose further parallelism: new frames arrive while earlier queries are still being answered. Engines differ in compute and memory access and contend when they co-run, so no single placement of stages wins across SoCs and workloads. Backend-local KV state keeps other engines from continuing the work. \name coordinates heterogeneous accelerators to execute stateful VLM inference concurrently. It separates a stream's versioned context from each engine's KV state, so any engine can continue the stream by reusing, importing, or reconstructing that state. Stateful Streaming Generation ingests new frames on one engine while another answers, and Concurrent Sequence Generation serves different ready requests in parallel. State-aware dispatch sends each request to the engine expected to finish it first. Each answer reads a fixed, published version of its history and matches its serial reference. \name beats the strongest single-engine baselines on Apple M4 Max, Rockchip RK3588, and Qualcomm QCS6490, even when they reuse context. On two RK3588 video streams, it delivers $19.6\%$ more throughput than the best single engine with context reuse and $27.2\%$ more than parallel engines without shared state. Cross-engine KV import cuts first-token latency by a factor of $4.6$ over prefix recomputation. Stateful Streaming Generation delivers up to $2.48\times$ the throughput of serial execution, and state-aware dispatch cuts query completion time by up to $72.7\%$.</p></details> |  |

| **[When to Stop Penalizing Actions? At these stops, the removed gradient contribution has zero conditional expectation given the retained information. Truncation therefore preserves the expectation of a reference GRPO group-gradient estimator and can reduce its covariance. One such stop occurs when the conditional probability of success is zero, motivating error and recovery cues for selecting candidate feedback boundaries. Martingale GRPO (MART-GRPO) is introduced with a vision-language model (VLM) for event-guided truncation. The VLM is fine-tuned on our annotated training set using low-rank adaptation (LoRA). Failure feedback is retained through the selected error interval and removed afterward, while successful-rollout loss terms and original loss normalization are preserved. > Visual prompting and reasoning methods that may support planning, navigation, and driving decisions.

| **[ZeroDex: Zero-Shot Long-Horizon Dexterous Manipulation via Multi-View 3D-Grounded VLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.19340v3)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We present ZeroDex, a zero-shot framework for long-horizon dexterous manipulation that grounds language instructions into executable 3D task plans from calibrated multi-view RGB images. The VLM decomposes a complex instruction into a sequence of atomic tasks, each executed by a primitive from a small reusable library that covers manipulation tasks including pick-and-place and tool-use: for tool-use, we retrieve an object-centric atomic action corresponding to the inferred skill category and align its stored 6D tool trajectory to the grounded keypoints; for dexterous execution, we expand the lifted grasp keypoint into a task-conditioned grasp affordance region and generate feasible grasp-motion pairs with an arm-hand motion generator. | **[Imagine Before You Draw: Visual Prompt Engineering for Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.04457v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Incorporating visual semantic representations as an intermediate step before image generation can reduce the modeling difficulty between text and images, thereby improving generation quality. For example, for a visual physics reasoning task ("Where does the ball land, after passing a set of obstacles?"), an abstract sketch-like scene can be turned into a photorealistic version with a simple call to an image editing model. We find that visual prompt engineering, or VIPE for short, improves video reasoning performance across tasks. In fact, for video models, visual prompt engineering can be even more effective than classic text-based prompt engineering or test-time scaling. Ultimately, just as text-based prompt engineering systematically improves language model performance, visual prompt engineering can serve as a simple, compute-efficient approach to elicit better visual reasoning performance from video models. Example videos on our project page at https://visual-prompt-engineering.github.io/.</p></details> |  |
| **[GAP-MLLM: Geometry-Aligned Pre-training for Activating 3D Spatial Perception in Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.16461v2)** | 2026-07-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) demonstrate exceptional semantic reasoning but struggle with 3D spatial perception when restricted to pure RGB inputs. Specifically, we introduce a visual-prompted joint task that compels the MLLMs to predict sparse pointmaps alongside semantic labels, thereby enforcing geometric awareness. Furthermore, we design a multi-level progressive fusion module with a token-level gating mechanism, enabling adaptive integration of geometric priors without suppressing semantic reasoning. Project page: https://gapmllm.github.io/</p></details> |

Experimental results demonstrate that SEVEX significantly outperforms baseline methods in task accuracy, inference efficiency, exploration efficiency, and exploration stability. | **[VPA-Guard: Defending and Benchmarking Image-to-Video Generation Against Visual Prompt Attacks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25592v1)** | 2026-06-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advancements in Image-to-Video (I2V) generation have transformed input images from simple appearance references into interactive control interfaces where visual cues such as arrows, sketches, and emojis orchestrate complex video dynamics with unprecedented controllability. ### World Models for Autonomous Driving

### Embodied AI for Navigation
> Embodied agents, embodied intelligence, and robot navigation methods relevant to autonomous systems.

| **[EmbodiedLab: Benchmarking Embodied Agents That Act to Know](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35385v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Embodied agents are expected to accomplish real-world goals by deciding for themselves how and when to interact with their surroundings. Manipulation benchmarks stop at acting to change, with the target state given; scientific discovery benchmarks evaluate knowing but replace physical experimentation with symbolic actions or numerical queries. We introduce EmbodiedLab, a benchmark in which a robot must act to know. Quantitative science experiments make this setting controllable: the parameters of the system are resampled for every instance, so the result cannot be known in advance, yet it can be scored exactly. Across 30 experiments in five disciplines on 14 apparatus platforms, the agent receives an objective and the apparatus, chooses its own procedure, and operates a physics-based simulated laboratory, reading the instruments through its cameras. Evaluation checks what the agent came to know, grounded in readings recorded during the episode, as well as the physical state the task requires; every experiment is verified to be solvable. We evaluate six frontier models in their developers' coding agents with native observation and control, with added perception and motion planning, and with manipulation skills distilled from the strongest agents. With native control, even the strongest agent succeeds less than half the time; for every agent, most failures arise in interacting with the apparatus, and manipulation skills help weaker agents more than perception and planning. The benchmark and its evaluation code are available at https://github.com/HexaFuture/EmbodiedLab.</p></details> | <details><summary>Techn...</summary><p>Technical report. 19 pages, 10 figures, 9 tables. Project page: https://hexafuture.ai/benchmarks/embodiedlab/</p></details> |

For example, an unsuccessful placement changes what must be done next, even when the experimental goal remains unchanged. Existing benchmarks assess planning, manipulation, and laboratory workflows through task completion and reasoning or execution metrics; however, aggregate performance leaves the connection between outcome-dependent choices and physical execution unresolved. To address this evaluation gap, we introduce Labference, a benchmark for evaluating how observed physical outcomes guide subsequent choices and how those choices translate into robotic laboratory actions. Its evaluation protocol is designed to test outcome-dependent behavioral adaptation through controlled evidence variations, while independently measuring operation selection, physical execution, and task completion. Matched Oracle-assisted continuations from shared checkpoints then assess which forms of support improve subsequent choices and task completion. Initial weighing evaluations reveal target-selection errors in the tested VLA policies despite successful object transfers, whereas GPT-driven control achieves correct selection but encounters execution failures and substantial computation costs. Together, these findings highlight the need to connect task-appropriate reasoning with reliable, timely action. By making this connection measurable and diagnosable, Labference supports targeted improvements toward autonomous experimentation for Embodied AI for Science.</p></details> |  |
| **[JRDB-AVR: An Active Visual Reasoning Benchmark for Embodied Agents in Real-World Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35032v2)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In complex embodied visual reasoning scenarios, an agent often has only a limited field of view, and the evidence needed to answer a question may be distributed across time, viewpoint, and interacting objects. We introduce JRDB-AVR, a benchmark derived from existing real-world JRDB robotics data through a structured question-generation engine that turns this gap into an explicit evaluation: an embodied agentic system receives a visual reasoning question, requests bounded observations by timestamp and viewing angle, and is evaluated on both the final answer and the grounded visual evidence supporting it. The benchmark contains diverse questions over multiple real-world environments involving temporal search, viewpoint selection, and human-oriented compositional reasoning. We also introduce JRDB-AVR-Agent, a reference active reasoning agentic method that maintains an explicit observation-grounded graph-based world model and answers through solving. | **[Are Frontier VLM Agents Ready to Be Robot Generalists? An Empirical Study with the Embodied Agent Arena](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00854v3)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A robot generalist must interpret a scene, act on it, and satisfy every condition of a task. During execution, agents can achieve early subgoals yet lose object control, bind an action to the wrong receiving region, or stop before the required physical state holds. Additional computation does not uniformly resolve these failures: code repair can restore execution without completing the task, while self-review can reverse correct judgments. | **[Physical AI: Concepts, Techniques, and Challenges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27045v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In recent years, physical artificial intelligence (physical AI) has experienced rapid development, capturing significant attention in academia and industry. To address this gap, this monograph seeks to define physical AI, present its technical landscape, and examine each key technical subfield in depth. In doing so, it establishes a unified framework for Physical AI: artificial systems that perceive, model, and act upon the physical world through continuous feedback under real-world constraints. Further, this monograph distinguishes physical AI from conventional digital AI, embodied AI, cyber-physical systems, and digital twins. By connecting perception, prediction, decision-making, action, and adaptation in a closed loop, this monograph identifies the technical challenges and research directions required to advance from narrow automation toward more generalizable physical AI and, ultimately, physical artificial general intelligence (Physical AGI).</p></details> | <details><summary>Monog...</summary><p>Monograph, 12 chapters, 388 pages, 64 figures</p></details> |
| **[Has GPT-6-Astra Achieved Embodied Intelligence?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22471v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>GPT-6-Astra demonstrates a level of competence in robotic manipulation and navigation that challenges the conventional paradigm of building specialized models for individual tasks and embodiments. In this report, we ask not only how capable GPT-6-Astra is, but what its capabilities imply for the field. First, we probe the limits of standalone GPT-6-Astra. We conduct a large-scale evaluation across 12 representative benchmarks spanning tabletop manipulation, humanoids, mobile platforms, and dexterous hands. Under a unified protocol that allows in-context learning (ICL) from training-set demonstrations and substantially increases the number of execution steps, GPT-6-Astra achieves unprecedented performance (Fig. 1). Future research may focus on improving 1) the generality of ICL 2) cost and execution time, 3) physical-state verification, 4) recovery from irreversible failures, and 5) long-horizon preservation of progress. Second, we study the agentic harness for embodied control. We identify that close-loop coordination between a high-level "System 2" reasoner (i.e., GPT-6-Astra) and a low-level "System 1" controller (e.g., π0.5) is costly but effective. Third, we examine GPT-6-Astra on real-world deployment. Our experiments on 8 robot configurations reveal substantial gaps: 1) robot demonstrations required for ICL are expensive to collect, and 2) it is implausible to trade longer execution for higher success rates with physical constraints. Fourth, we envision the next generation of embodied benchmarks. We develop a prototype that 1) derives tasks from real-world needs, 2) provides no in-domain demonstrations, and 3) judges agents on a world clock that keeps running while they deliberate. Authors are ordered alphabetically by their surnames</p></details> |
| **[PVBench: Quantifying Physical Plausibility in Video Generation with Object-Level Physical Measurements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26711v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent video generation models produce visually compelling outputs, yet their physical plausibility remains poorly measured. We introduce PVBench, a physical video benchmark that quantitatively measures the plausibility of generated videos across 46 dimensions organized into seven domains, spanning: i) instruction and semantic fidelity, ii) behavioral and causal consistency, iii) perceptual and temporal quality, iv) geometric and spatial consistency, v) material properties and field states, vi) kinematic and spatiotemporal persistence, vii) and mechanical plausibility with conservation laws. Finally, we validate the agreement between automatic metrics and human judgments through human evaluation. | **[ForagerSim: Evaluating Embodied Map-Based Reasoning in Large-Scale Dynamic Worlds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26395v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Animals build cognitive maps of vast environments, use them to perform map-based navigation, and might even be reusing the same circuitry used in spatial reasoning for creating and using maps of arbitrary task-spaces. Exploring Emergent Navigation from Web-Scale Knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.15933v4)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Leveraging multimodal large language models (MLLMs) to develop embodied agents offers significant promise for addressing complex real-world tasks. | **[DialogMem: Evaluating Dialogue-Grounded Embodied Memory in 3D Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24767v1)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Embodied agents in human-centered environments require memory that integrates what they observe with what humans tell them over time. | **[SpaAct: Spatially-Activated Transition Learning with Curriculum Adaptation for Vision-Language Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.27620v2)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision-and-Language Navigation (VLN) aims to enable an embodied agent to follow natural-language instructions and navigate to a target location in unseen 3D environments. | **[Refuse the Harm, Complete the Task via Graph-of-Rules Planning for LLM-Enabled Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24603v1)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used in embodied AI for robotic manipulation and planning, but their deployment in physical systems exposes vulnerabilities such as jailbreak attacks that induce unsafe behavior. We introduce safe task completion, a task that requires an LLM-enabled robot to refuse the harmful part of an instruction while completing its legitimate part. Planning toward this goal is then organized as a graph of rule-specific nodes, where higher-level nodes inherit the union of their parents' rules, and candidate plans are progressively verified and aggregated until a single plan satisfies every relevant rule. We evaluate RuleGraph on pure-attack and compound instructions across two benchmarks in two environments using Attack Success Rate (ASR) and Task Success Rate (TSR). | **[CRAFT-E: An Interpretable and Editable Neuro-Symbolic Framework for Affordance-Grounded Active Object Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.04231v2)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Assistive robots operating in unstructured environments must reason not only about what objects are, but how they can be used, including non-canonical and improvised uses. Existing approaches often rely on black-box models, fixed affordance labels, or direct object predictions, limiting transparency and controllability. We introduce CRAFT-E, a modular neuro-symbolic framework that composes an LLM-derived verb-property-object knowledge graph with CLIP-based visual alignment and energy-based grasp reasoning. The system exposes interpretable grounding paths, incorporates grasp feasibility into affordance inference, and supports targeted graph edits without retraining. We also construct a benchmark dataset with unified annotations for verb-object compatibility, segmentation, and grasp candidates, and deploy the full pipeline on a physical robot. Across static scenes, real-world trials involving 20 verbs and 39 objects, ImageNet-based functional retrieval, and COCO-Tasks transfer, CRAFT-E provides a competitive training-free prior for affordance-grounded retrieval while exposing component-level diagnostics and graph-level interventions. Under staged and full-pipeline perception, CRAFT-E matches strong flat LLM priors in several settings while adding failure attribution and editable reasoning paths. By coupling symbolic reasoning with embodied perception, CRAFT-E offers an inspectable and customizable framework for affordance-grounded object retrieval.</p></details> | <details><summary>WACV ...</summary><p>WACV 2027. | **[GenZ-LIO: Generalizable LiDAR-Inertial Odometry Beyond Confined--Open Boundaries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.16273v3)** | 2026-10-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>For field robotic missions such as inspection, search-and-rescue, and exploration, light detection and ranging (LiDAR)-inertial odometry (LIO) can serve as a core component of autonomy by providing localization and mapping in GNSS-denied or unstructured environments. To address these issues, we present GenZ-LIO, a generalizable LIO framework designed to adapt to variations in spatial scale across confined and open environments. GenZ-LIO comprises three components: (i) scale-aware adaptive voxelization for regulating scan downsampling across spatial-scale changes, (ii) hybrid-metric state update for combining point-to-plane and point-to-point residuals under varying geometric structure, and (iii) voxel-pruned correspondence search for efficient point-to-point matching. We conduct a comprehensive evaluation using 42 sequences from nine public datasets and our newly collected NarrowWide dataset to analyze LIO performance under spatial-scale variations across diverse field scenarios. Across the evaluated sequences, GenZ-LIO maintains stable odometry estimation without divergence, indicating practical robustness under the tested field conditions. Our code is available at https://github.com/cocel-postech/genz-lio .</p></details> | <details><summary>29 pa...</summary><p>29 pages, 19 figures, Accepted to IEEE Transactions on Field Robotics (T-FR)</p></details> |
| **[AGT-CV: An Aerial-Ground Team Cross-View Dataset for Heterogeneous Robot Teams in Unstructured Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06478v2)** | 2026-10-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Heterogeneous air-ground robot teams combine complementary sensing modalities, mobility characteristics, and spatial viewpoints that can significantly enhance perception in complex outdoor environments. | **[Rectify, Don't Regret: On-Policy Closed-Loop Training for Multimodal Trajectory Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.23393v2)** | 2026-09-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Current trajectory prediction models are primarily trained in an open-loop manner, which often leads to covariate shift and compounding errors when deployed in real-world, closed-loop settings. We show empirically that this shortcut couples the learned policy to the specific replanning rate used during training and degrades at other rates, whereas detaching the gradient forces the model to rectify drift instead, allowing it to generalize across replanning rates unseen during training. | **[Towards a Unified Understanding of Robot Manipulation: A Comprehensive Survey](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.10903v2)** | 2026-08-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Embodied intelligence has witnessed remarkable progress in recent years, driven by advances in computer vision, natural language processing, and the rise of large-scale multimodal models. ### Scene Captioning for Driving
> Scene captioning, multimodal routing, and language-conditioned scene understanding for driving.

| **[Beyond Being Accurate: Implications for Using Natural Language Descriptions for Accessible 3D Modeling with~Blind and Low Vision Makers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35562v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>People who are blind or have low vision (BLV) are largely excluded from 3D modeling tools that rely on visual representations of geometry. Natural language descriptions offer a potential path to accessibility, but it is unclear whether language alone can support the deep spatial understanding that editing, not just browsing, requires. Advances in generative AI offer the promise of automatic model description, yet descriptions suited to BLV modelers are structurally distinct from alt-text and from existing 3D model captioning datasets, and the domain-specific requirements for generating them are not well understood. We address this through two coordinated studies: an autoethnographic pilot in which the blind first author worked with 12 sighted participants to develop a taxonomy of descriptive strategies, and a Wizard-of-Oz main study that applied those strategies as idealized scripts with 10 BLV participants. We identify a systematic use-to-editing gap: descriptions that support accurate but incomplete mental models are insufficient for specifying modifications, because editing requires knowing the state of features the user did not know to ask about. We show that this gap produces four failure types (accuracy, completeness, expression, and navigation) that accuracy-focused evaluation frameworks cannot detect. We propose a two-phase evaluation framework that diagnoses the failure type a description system exhibits and guides developers toward targeted design interventions, thereby providing a methodological standard for assessing whether description-based systems genuinely support BLV modelers as creators.</p></details> |  |

A shared BLIP encoder drives scene captioning and text-conditioned segmentation, which is fused with depth to localize obstacles in 3D. The perception pipeline runs at 10\,Hz on a Jetson Orin NX, while fallback LLM queries take approximately 0.8\,s and remain off the control-critical path. In Gazebo, low-altitude urban navigation among pedestrians and vehicles shows a roughly $3\times$ reduction in collision events over a geometric planner, and semantics-aware landing among moving actors raises success from 34\% to 96\%. | **[Multi-View Relational Distillation for Spatial Reasoning with Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10864v2)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision-language models (VLMs) have achieved strong image and video understanding, yet their visual-spatial representations remain geometrically fragile, leading to failures in spatial reasoning needed for embodied AI, robotics, and autonomous driving. (2) Convergent content addressing: identical content yields identical ciphertext and identifiers, enabling deduplication without plaintext exposure, with identifiers binding authenticated ciphertext so a keyless Drop verifies integrity (blind verifiability). (3) Three parallel trees over one navigation path (Merkle for integrity, key-derivation for confidentiality, access for authorisation), with sound Merkle-verified retrieval. (4) The key tree doubles as a streaming index: a player derives each segment key in O(1), seeking by derivation, while parallel tracks (video, audio, captions) are independent subtrees unlockable per-track and per-segment, a combination we believe no prior encrypted-storage network offers. (5) Jets and Drops earn Safebux verifiably, kept honest by a one-signature proof-of-storage challenge under chilling-effect Proof-of-Corruption, a zero-sum economy that is significantly cheaper than Filecoin's proof-of-replication sealing (which is slow and provides no confidentiality). We give the architecture, cryptographic construction, a threat model, and an open-source reference implementation, stating precisely what is implemented versus designed.</p></details> | <details><summary>7 pag...</summary><p>7 pages, 2 tables. Reference implementation open-source. Companion to Intercloud (arXiv:2605.22830) and a forthcoming Safecloud 2.0 compute paper</p></details> |
| **[Unveiling the Fragility of Vision-Language Models: Multi-Modal Adversarial Synergy via Texture-Constrained Perturbations and Cross-Modal Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26501v1)** | 2026-05-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs) have transformed multi-modal understanding, excelling in tasks like image captioning and visual question answering by integrating visual and textual inputs. The image perturbation leverages wavelet-based texture constraints to ensure imperceptibility and robustness across diverse visual inputs. The text perturbation, constrained by an L-norm in the embedding space, maintains semantic coherence while steering outputs toward a target. A novel cross-modal regularization term aligns the perturbations' gradient directions, enhancing their synergistic impact and transferability across tasks and models. To optimize SAM2 for range-view representations, we implement several architectural modifications to the encoder: (1) a novel module that emphasizes horizontal spatial dependencies inherent in LiDAR range images, (2) a customized configuration of tailored to the geometric properties of spherical projections, and (3) an adapted mechanism in the encoder backbone specifically designed to capture the unique spatial patterns and discontinuities present in range-view pseudo-images. Our approach achieves competitive performance on SemanticKITTI while benefiting from the speed, scalability, and deployment simplicity of 2D-centric pipelines. This work highlights the viability of VFMs as general-purpose backbones for 3D perception and opens a path toward unified, foundation-model-driven LiDAR segmentation. Results lets us conclude that range-view segmentation methods using VFMs leads to promising results.</p></details> |  |
| **[When Proximity Falls Short: Inequalities in Commuting and Accessibility by Public Transport in Santiago, Chile](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.21743v2)** | 2026-01-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Traditional measures of urban accessibility often rely on static models or survey data. However, location information from mobile networks now enables large-scale, dynamic analyses of how people navigate cities. This study uses eXtended Detail Records (XDRs) derived from mobile phone activity to analyze commuting patterns and accessibility inequalities in Santiago, Chile. First, we identify residential and work locations and model commuting routes using the R5 multimodal routing engine, which combines public transport and walking. To explore spatial patterns, we apply a bivariate spatial clustering analysis (LISA) alongside regression techniques to identify distinct commuting behaviors and their alignment with vulnerable population groups. Our findings reveal that average commuting times remain consistent across socioeconomic groups. However, despite residing in areas with greater opportunity density, higher-income populations do not consistently experience shorter commuting times. This highlights a disconnect between spatial proximity to opportunities and actual travel experience. Our analysis reveals significant disparities between sociodemographic groups, particularly regarding the distribution of indigenous populations and gender. Overall, the findings of our study suggest that commuting and accessibility inequalities in Santiago are closely linked to broader social and demographic structures.</p></details> | <details><summary>30 pa...</summary><p>30 pages, Journal Paper</p></details> |
| **[Five Years of SciCap: What We Learned and Future Directions for Scientific Figure Captioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.21789v2)** | 2026-01-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Between 2021 and 2025, the SciCap project grew from a small seed-funded idea at The Pennsylvania State University (Penn State) into one of the central efforts shaping the scientific figure-captioning landscape. Supported by a Penn State seed grant, Adobe, and the Alfred P. Sloan Foundation, what began as our attempt to test whether domain-specific training, which was successful in text models like SciBERT, could also work for figure captions expanded into a multi-institution collaboration. Over these five years, we curated, released, and continually updated a large collection of figure-caption pairs from arXiv papers, conducted extensive automatic and human evaluations on both generated and author-written captions, navigated the rapid rise of large language models (LLMs), launched annual challenges, and built interactive systems that help scientists write better captions. In this piece, we look back at the first five years of SciCap and summarize the key technical and methodological lessons we learned. We then outline five major unsolved challenges and propose directions for the next phase of research in scientific figure captioning.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to the 5th Annual AAAI Workshop on AI to Accelerate Science and Engineering (AI2ASE 2026). SciCap Website: http://scicap.ai/</p></details> |
| **[3D-Agent:Tri-Modal Multi-Agent Collaboration for Scalable 3D Object Annotation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.04404v1)** | 2026-01-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Driven by applications in autonomous driving robotics and augmented reality 3D object annotation presents challenges beyond 2D annotation including spatial complexity occlusion and viewpoint inconsistency Existing approaches based on single models often struggle to address these issues effectively We propose Tri MARF a novel framework that integrates tri modal inputs including 2D multi view images textual descriptions and 3D point clouds within a multi agent collaborative architecture to enhance large scale 3D annotation Tri MARF consists of three specialized agents a vision language model agent for generating multi view descriptions an information aggregation agent for selecting optimal descriptions and a gating agent that aligns textual semantics with 3D geometry for refined captioning Extensive experiments on Objaverse LVIS Objaverse XL and ABO demonstrate that Tri MARF substantially outperforms existing methods achieving a CLIPScore of 88 point 7 compared to prior state of the art methods retrieval accuracy of 45 point 2 and 43 point 8 on ViLT R at 5 and a throughput of up to 12000 objects per hour on a single NVIDIA A100 GPU</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2025</p></details> |
